*Beyond the lectures · Ho, Jain & Abbeel, NeurIPS 2020 ([arXiv:2006.11239](https://arxiv.org/abs/2006.11239)) · [[8-diffusion-models/index|Diffusion models]] · builds on [[8-diffusion-models/brownian-motion-and-forward-diffusion|the forward noising process]] and [[8-diffusion-models/reverse-time-sde-and-score-matching|the reverse-time SDE and the score]]*

> [!abstract] In one breath
> Fix a forward chain that adds a little Gaussian noise at each of $T = 1000$ steps until the data is pure noise, and train **one** network $\epsilon_\theta(x_t, t)$ to predict the noise that was added: pick a data point, a step $t$ and a noise vector $\epsilon$, form $x_t = \sqrt{\bar\alpha_t}\,x_0 + \sqrt{1-\bar\alpha_t}\,\epsilon$ in one shot, and minimize $\lVert \epsilon - \epsilon_\theta(x_t, t)\rVert^2$. To generate, start from $x_T \sim \mathcal N(0, I)$ and walk the chain backwards, at each step subtracting the predicted noise and adding a little fresh noise. Ho et al. showed this simple recipe gives image quality competitive with GANs (CIFAR-10 FID 3.17); its cost is $T$ network evaluations per sample, which the [[8-diffusion-models/fast-sampling-ddim-and-ode-solvers|fast samplers]] attack.

## What it is

A **diffusion probabilistic model** (Sohl-Dickstein et al., 2015) is a latent-variable model whose latents $x_1, \dots, x_T$ have the same shape as the data $x_0$. Two Markov chains run in opposite directions:

> [!definition] Definition — forward and reverse processes (Ho et al., eqs. 1–2)
> **Forward (fixed, no parameters):** $q(x_t \mid x_{t-1}) = \mathcal N\!\bigl(x_t;\ \sqrt{1-\beta_t}\,x_{t-1},\ \beta_t I\bigr)$ for a variance schedule $\beta_1, \dots, \beta_T$.
>
> **Reverse (learned):** $p_\theta(x_{0:T}) = p(x_T)\prod_{t=1}^{T} p_\theta(x_{t-1}\mid x_t)$ with $p(x_T) = \mathcal N(0, I)$ and
> $$
> p_\theta(x_{t-1}\mid x_t) = \mathcal N\!\bigl(x_{t-1};\ \mu_\theta(x_t, t),\ \Sigma_\theta(x_t, t)\bigr).
> $$
> With $\alpha_t := 1-\beta_t$ and $\bar\alpha_t := \prod_{s=1}^{t}\alpha_s$, the forward process has the closed form $q(x_t\mid x_0) = \mathcal N\bigl(x_t;\ \sqrt{\bar\alpha_t}\,x_0,\ (1-\bar\alpha_t)I\bigr)$ (eq. 4).

Each reverse step only has to undo a *small* amount of Gaussian noise, and when $\beta_t$ is small the true reverse step is itself close to Gaussian, which is why a Gaussian $p_\theta$ with a learned mean is enough. The forward process is the discrete version of the [[8-diffusion-models/brownian-motion-and-forward-diffusion|Brownian-motion noising]]; the learned reverse chain is a discretization of the [[8-diffusion-models/reverse-time-sde-and-score-matching|reverse-time SDE]].

## What you should know

- The closed form $x_t = \sqrt{\bar\alpha_t}\,x_0 + \sqrt{1-\bar\alpha_t}\,\epsilon$, and that it lets training jump straight to any $t$ without simulating the chain.
- The forward-process posterior $q(x_{t-1}\mid x_t, x_0)$: Gaussian with mean $\tilde\mu_t$ and variance $\tilde\beta_t = \frac{1-\bar\alpha_{t-1}}{1-\bar\alpha_t}\beta_t$.
- The **ε-parameterization** $\mu_\theta(x_t, t) = \frac{1}{\sqrt{\alpha_t}}\bigl(x_t - \frac{\beta_t}{\sqrt{1-\bar\alpha_t}}\,\epsilon_\theta(x_t, t)\bigr)$, and how it follows from substituting the predicted $x_0$ into $\tilde\mu_t$.
- $L_{\text{simple}} = \mathbb E_{t, x_0, \epsilon}\lVert\epsilon - \epsilon_\theta(\sqrt{\bar\alpha_t}x_0 + \sqrt{1-\bar\alpha_t}\epsilon, t)\rVert^2$: an unweighted version of the variational bound; it gave the best samples.
- Algorithms 1 (training) and 2 (sampling) line by line, including $z = 0$ at the last step and $\sigma_t^2 \in \{\beta_t, \tilde\beta_t\}$.
- The paper's settings: $T = 1000$, linear $\beta_t$ from $10^{-4}$ to $0.02$, a U-Net with a sinusoidal time embedding and self-attention at $16\times16$.
- Sampling needs $T$ **sequential** network evaluations; that is the main weakness.
- $\epsilon_\theta$ is a scaled estimate of the score: $\nabla_x \log p_t(x) \approx -\epsilon_\theta(x, t)/\sqrt{1-\bar\alpha_t}$.

DDPM's linear schedule (β from 10<sup>−4</sup> to 0.02 over 1000 steps) leaves almost no signal for the last quarter of the chain; the cosine schedule spends the steps more evenly. The toy model uses 100 steps with the linear β range scaled by 10 so that the total noise matches (its ᾱ<sub>100</sub> ≈ 2·10<sup>−5</sup>).</figcaption></figure>

With the paper's linear schedule, $\sum_t \beta_t = 10.05$ and $\bar\alpha_{1000} = 4.0\times10^{-5}$: almost nothing of $x_0$ is left, so $q(x_T\mid x_0) \approx \mathcal N(0, I)$ whatever the image was. The paper reports the prior-matching term $L_T = D_{\mathrm{KL}}(q(x_T\mid x_0)\,\Vert\,\mathcal N(0, I)) \approx 10^{-5}$ bits per dimension. Halfway through ($t = 500$), $\bar\alpha_t \approx 0.079$, and by $t = 750$ it is $0.0034$ — the motivation for Nichol & Dhariwal's **cosine schedule** $\bar\alpha_t = f(t)/f(0)$, $f(t) = \cos^2\!\bigl(\frac{t/T + s}{1+s}\cdot\frac{\pi}{2}\bigr)$ with $s = 0.008$ and $\beta_t$ clipped at $0.999$, which destroys information more gradually.

## Reversing one step: the posterior

The true reverse step $q(x_{t-1}\mid x_t)$ needs the unknown data distribution, but conditioned on $x_0$ it is a Gaussian we can write down (eqs. 6–7):

$$
q(x_{t-1}\mid x_t, x_0) = \mathcal N\bigl(x_{t-1};\ \tilde\mu_t(x_t, x_0),\ \tilde\beta_t I\bigr),\qquad
\tilde\mu_t = \frac{\sqrt{\bar\alpha_{t-1}}\,\beta_t}{1-\bar\alpha_t}\,x_0 + \frac{\sqrt{\alpha_t}\,(1-\bar\alpha_{t-1})}{1-\bar\alpha_t}\,x_t,\qquad
\tilde\beta_t = \frac{1-\bar\alpha_{t-1}}{1-\bar\alpha_t}\,\beta_t .
$$

> [!derivation]- Derivation — Bayes' rule with two Gaussians
> By Bayes, $q(x_{t-1}\mid x_t, x_0) \propto q(x_t\mid x_{t-1})\,q(x_{t-1}\mid x_0)$ (the forward chain is Markov, so $x_0$ drops out of the first factor). As a function of $x_{t-1}$ both factors are Gaussian:
> $q(x_t\mid x_{t-1}) \propto \exp\bigl(-\frac{(x_t - \sqrt{\alpha_t}x_{t-1})^2}{2\beta_t}\bigr)$ has precision $\alpha_t/\beta_t$ in $x_{t-1}$ and pulls toward $x_t/\sqrt{\alpha_t}$; $q(x_{t-1}\mid x_0)$ has precision $1/(1-\bar\alpha_{t-1})$ and mean $\sqrt{\bar\alpha_{t-1}}x_0$. Precisions add:
> $$
> \frac{1}{\tilde\beta_t} = \frac{\alpha_t}{\beta_t} + \frac{1}{1-\bar\alpha_{t-1}} = \frac{\alpha_t(1-\bar\alpha_{t-1}) + \beta_t}{\beta_t(1-\bar\alpha_{t-1})} = \frac{1-\bar\alpha_t}{\beta_t(1-\bar\alpha_{t-1})},
> $$
> using $\alpha_t\bar\alpha_{t-1} = \bar\alpha_t$ and $\alpha_t + \beta_t = 1$. The mean is the precision-weighted average $\tilde\mu_t = \tilde\beta_t\bigl(\frac{\sqrt{\alpha_t}}{\beta_t}x_t + \frac{\sqrt{\bar\alpha_{t-1}}}{1-\bar\alpha_{t-1}}x_0\bigr)$, which multiplies out to the formula above. (A numerical check with the paper's schedule: fix $x_0 = 0.5$, simulate 2 million pairs $(x_{299}, x_{300})$ and keep those with $x_{300}$ within 0.005 of 0.2; their $x_{299}$ have mean 0.203 and variance 0.00604, against $\tilde\mu_{300} = 0.202$ and $\tilde\beta_{300} = 0.00603$.)

## The training objective

**The variational bound.** Like a VAE, the model is trained on an upper bound of $-\log p_\theta(x_0)$, which Ho et al. rewrite as a sum of KL divergences (eq. 5):

$$
L = \mathbb E_q\Bigl[\underbrace{D_{\mathrm{KL}}\bigl(q(x_T\mid x_0)\,\Vert\,p(x_T)\bigr)}_{L_T} + \sum_{t>1}\underbrace{D_{\mathrm{KL}}\bigl(q(x_{t-1}\mid x_t, x_0)\,\Vert\,p_\theta(x_{t-1}\mid x_t)\bigr)}_{L_{t-1}}\ \underbrace{-\,\log p_\theta(x_0\mid x_1)}_{L_0}\Bigr].
$$

In words: the last latent must look like the prior ($L_T$, a constant once $\beta_t$ is fixed), every learned reverse step must match the tractable posterior ($L_{t-1}$, a KL between two Gaussians, so closed-form), and the final step must reconstruct the data ($L_0$, a discretized Gaussian decoder over the 256 pixel values).

**Fixed variances.** Ho et al. set $\Sigma_\theta = \sigma_t^2 I$ to untrained constants and found $\sigma_t^2 = \beta_t$ and $\sigma_t^2 = \tilde\beta_t$ gave similar results; they are the optimal choices for the two extreme cases $x_0 \sim \mathcal N(0, I)$ and $x_0$ fixed to one point. Learning a diagonal $\Sigma_\theta$ was unstable in their experiments (Nichol & Dhariwal later made it work by interpolating between the two, $\Sigma_\theta = \exp(v\log\beta_t + (1-v)\log\tilde\beta_t)$).

**Predicting the noise.** With fixed variances, $L_{t-1} = \mathbb E\bigl[\frac{1}{2\sigma_t^2}\lVert\tilde\mu_t(x_t, x_0) - \mu_\theta(x_t, t)\rVert^2\bigr] + C$ (eq. 8). Writing $x_t = \sqrt{\bar\alpha_t}x_0 + \sqrt{1-\bar\alpha_t}\epsilon$ and substituting $x_0 = (x_t - \sqrt{1-\bar\alpha_t}\epsilon)/\sqrt{\bar\alpha_t}$ into $\tilde\mu_t$ gives

$$
\tilde\mu_t = \frac{1}{\sqrt{\alpha_t}}\Bigl(x_t - \frac{\beta_t}{\sqrt{1-\bar\alpha_t}}\,\epsilon\Bigr),
$$

so the network only has to predict $\epsilon$ (eq. 11):

$$
\mu_\theta(x_t, t) = \frac{1}{\sqrt{\alpha_t}}\Bigl(x_t - \frac{\beta_t}{\sqrt{1-\bar\alpha_t}}\,\epsilon_\theta(x_t, t)\Bigr).
$$

Each $L_{t-1}$ then becomes a weighted noise-regression loss $\frac{\beta_t^2}{2\sigma_t^2\alpha_t(1-\bar\alpha_t)}\lVert\epsilon - \epsilon_\theta\rVert^2$ (eq. 12), which is **denoising score matching** at noise level $t$ — the bridge to [[8-diffusion-models/reverse-time-sde-and-score-matching|score-based models]].

**Dropping the weights.** The paper's simplified objective (eq. 14) throws the weights away:

$$
L_{\text{simple}}(\theta) = \mathbb E_{t\sim\mathcal U\{1..T\},\ x_0,\ \epsilon\sim\mathcal N(0,I)}\Bigl[\bigl\lVert \epsilon - \epsilon_\theta\bigl(\sqrt{\bar\alpha_t}\,x_0 + \sqrt{1-\bar\alpha_t}\,\epsilon,\ t\bigr)\bigr\rVert^2\Bigr].
$$

Compared with the true bound it down-weights small $t$ (easy, almost-noise-free denoising) so the network spends its capacity on the harder large-$t$ steps. The ablation (Table 2, CIFAR-10 FID, lower is better):

| Predict | Objective | FID |
| --- | --- | --- |
| $\tilde\mu$ | true bound $L$, learned diagonal $\Sigma$ | 23.69 |
| $\tilde\mu$ | true bound $L$, fixed $\Sigma$ | 13.22 |
| $\tilde\mu$ | unweighted MSE on $\tilde\mu$ | unstable |
| $\epsilon$ | true bound $L$, fixed $\Sigma$ | 13.51 |
| $\epsilon$ | $L_{\text{simple}}$ | **3.17** |

The price: $L_{\text{simple}}$ is not a likelihood bound, and the $L_{\text{simple}}$ model's codelength ($\le 3.75$ bits/dim) is slightly worse than the bound-trained one ($\le 3.70$).

## Algorithms 1 and 2

> [!recipe] Algorithm 1 — Training (Ho et al.)
> 1. **repeat**
> 2. $\quad x_0 \sim q(x_0)$
> 3. $\quad t \sim \mathrm{Uniform}(\{1, \dots, T\})$
> 4. $\quad \epsilon \sim \mathcal N(0, I)$
> 5. $\quad$ take a gradient descent step on $\nabla_\theta \bigl\lVert \epsilon - \epsilon_\theta(\sqrt{\bar\alpha_t}\,x_0 + \sqrt{1-\bar\alpha_t}\,\epsilon,\ t)\bigr\rVert^2$
> 6. **until** converged

> [!recipe] Algorithm 2 — Sampling (Ho et al.)
> 1. $x_T \sim \mathcal N(0, I)$
> 2. **for** $t = T, \dots, 1$ **do**
> 3. $\quad z \sim \mathcal N(0, I)$ if $t > 1$, else $z = 0$
> 4. $\quad x_{t-1} = \dfrac{1}{\sqrt{\alpha_t}}\Bigl(x_t - \dfrac{1-\alpha_t}{\sqrt{1-\bar\alpha_t}}\,\epsilon_\theta(x_t, t)\Bigr) + \sigma_t z$
> 5. **end for**
> 6. **return** $x_0$

Note $1 - \alpha_t = \beta_t$, so line 4 is $\mu_\theta(x_t, t) + \sigma_t z$. Each pass through the loop is one network evaluation, and they must run in order: **1000 sequential evaluations per sample** at $T = 1000$. The paper's CIFAR-10 model samples a batch of 256 images in 17 seconds on a TPU v3-8; the DDIM paper estimates about 20 hours for 50k CIFAR-10 images on one RTX 2080 Ti.

> [!intuition] Why line 4 looks like Langevin dynamics
> Since $\epsilon_\theta(x, t) \approx -\sqrt{1-\bar\alpha_t}\,\nabla_x\log p_t(x)$, line 4 is roughly $x_{t-1} \approx \frac{1}{\sqrt{\alpha_t}}\bigl(x_t + \beta_t\nabla\log p_t(x_t)\bigr) + \sigma_t z$: a small step **up** the gradient of the log-density plus noise — the Langevin-dynamics update, run with a noise level that shrinks as $t$ decreases. This is the connection Ho et al. list as a main contribution.

## The network: a U-Net with a time embedding

$\epsilon_\theta$ maps an image and a step number to an image-shaped noise estimate, so it is a dense-prediction network like the [[5-deep-networks/fully-convolutional-networks|encoder–decoders and U-Nets]] used for segmentation. From the paper's Appendix B:

| Part | Choice |
| --- | --- |
| Backbone | U-Net like an unmasked PixelCNN++, built from Wide-ResNet [[5-deep-networks/residual-connections|residual blocks]]; group normalization instead of weight norm |
| Resolutions | 4 feature-map resolutions for $32\times32$ (32 down to 4), 6 for $256\times256$; two residual blocks per resolution |
| Attention | [[7-transformers/attention|self-attention]] blocks at the $16\times16$ resolution |
| Time $t$ | the Transformer [[7-transformers/positional-encoding|sinusoidal position embedding]] of $t$, added into each residual block; one set of weights for all $t$ |
| Size | 35.7M parameters (CIFAR-10), 114M (LSUN, CelebA-HQ), about 256M for a larger LSUN Bedroom model |
| Training | Adam, learning rate $2\times10^{-4}$ ($2\times10^{-5}$ at $256\times256$), batch 128 (64 at $256^2$), dropout 0.1 on CIFAR-10, random horizontal flips, EMA of weights with decay 0.9999; CIFAR-10 trained 800k steps (10.6 hours on a TPU v3-8) |
| Data | pixels scaled linearly from $\{0, \dots, 255\}$ to $[-1, 1]$ |

## Results

| Dataset | Metric | DDPM ($L_{\text{simple}}$) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| CIFAR-10, unconditional | FID (train set) / IS | **3.17** / 9.46 (FID 5.24 against the test set) |
| CIFAR-10 | NLL bound | $\le 3.75$ bits/dim (train 3.72) |
| LSUN Bedroom $256^2$ | FID | 6.36 (4.90 with the larger model) |
| LSUN Church $256^2$ | FID | 7.89 |
| LSUN Cat $256^2$ | FID | 19.75 |

At the time, 3.17 was better than most models in the literature, including class-conditional GANs; StyleGAN2 + ADA reached 2.67 conditional and 3.26 unconditional in the same table. The log-likelihoods were *not* competitive with other likelihood models: the authors find that more than half of the codelength describes imperceptible detail, and describe diffusion models as excellent lossy compressors. Sampling progressively shows coarse structure first and detail last.

## A tiny DDPM you can run (numpy)

To see every moving part without a GPU, the figures below come from a DDPM trained **in numpy, with hand-written backpropagation**, on 2-D "two moons" data (tools/diffusion_toy.py; gradients checked against finite differences to a relative error of $1.5\times10^{-7}$). Settings: $T = 100$ with the linear $\beta$ range multiplied by $1000/T = 10$ (so $\beta_t$ runs from $10^{-3}$ to $0.2$ and $\bar\alpha_{100} \approx 2\times10^{-5}$, the same total noise as the paper's 1000 steps); $\epsilon_\theta$ is an MLP on $[x_t, \text{emb}(t)]$ with a 16-dimensional sinusoidal time embedding, three hidden layers of 128 SiLU units and 35,714 parameters; Adam at $10^{-3}$, batch 256, 20,000 steps (about 80 seconds on a CPU).

The schedule, the time embedding and one training step:

```python
T = 100                                     # diffusion steps
SCALE = 1000 / T                            # keep total noise like T=1000
BETAS = np.linspace(SCALE * 1e-4, SCALE * 0.02, T)   # beta_1..beta_T
ALPHAS = 1.0 - BETAS
ALPHA_BAR = np.cumprod(ALPHAS)              # alpha_bar_1..alpha_bar_T

def time_embedding(t):
    """Sinusoidal embedding of integer steps t."""
    half = EMB // 2
    freqs = np.exp(-np.log(10000.0) * np.arange(half) / half)
    ang = np.asarray(t, float)[:, None] * freqs[None, :]
    return np.concatenate([np.sin(ang), np.cos(ang)], axis=1)

# inside the training loop (Algorithm 1)
x0 = data[rng.integers(0, n_data, batch)]           # x0 ~ q(x0)
t = rng.integers(1, T + 1, batch)                    # t ~ U{1..T}
eps = rng.standard_normal(x0.shape)                  # eps ~ N(0, I)
ab = ALPHA_BAR[t - 1][:, None]
xt = np.sqrt(ab) * x0 + np.sqrt(1 - ab) * eps        # q(x_t | x0)
loss, grads = loss_and_grads(params, xt, t, eps)     # L_simple and backprop
# then one Adam update per parameter
```

The sampler is Algorithm 2 with $\sigma_t^2 = \beta_t$:

```python
def sample_ddpm(params, n, rng, keep=()):
    """Algorithm 2 with sigma_t^2 = beta_t."""
    x = rng.standard_normal((n, 2))                          # x_T
    snaps = {T: x.copy()}
    for t in range(T, 0, -1):
        z = rng.standard_normal(x.shape) if t > 1 else 0.0
        eps = forward(params, x, np.full(n, t))
        a, ab, beta = ALPHAS[t - 1], ALPHA_BAR[t - 1], BETAS[t - 1]
        x = (x - beta / np.sqrt(1 - ab) * eps) / np.sqrt(a) + np.sqrt(beta) * z
        if t - 1 in keep:
            snaps[t - 1] = x.copy()
    return x, snaps
```

Adam, 20,000 steps of 256 points; grey = per-batch loss, blue = 200-step average). L<sub>simple</sub> falls from about 0.63 to 0.45, not to 0: at large t the noise cannot be predicted from x<sub>t</sub>, so a perfect model still has a large loss there. The loss is a poor guide to sample quality.</figcaption></figure>

held-out points of the two-moons data (1,200 of each drawn). Energy distance to the held-out set (unbiased estimate, 2,000-point subsamples): 0.0005. Fresh real samples score about 0 ± 0.0007, so the model is within the noise of the metric.</figcaption></figure>

Each panel is the whole batch at one step.</figcaption></figure>

width="162.33" height="162.33"/></clipPath><clipPath id="f8752e2"><rect x="201.99" y="21.56" width="162.33" height="162.33"/></clipPath></defs></svg> <figcaption><b>What the network learned: −ε<sub>θ</sub>(x, t)/√(1 − ᾱ<sub>t</sub>), the estimated score ∇ log p<sub>t</sub>(x)</b> (arrows normalized to unit length). At t = 50 the noisy distribution is one blob and every arrow points to its center; at t = 8 the arrows point onto the two moons. Each reverse step moves a point a little along these arrows and adds fresh noise.</figcaption></figure>

The energy distance used above is $2\,\mathbb E\lVert X - Y\rVert - \mathbb E\lVert X - X'\rVert - \mathbb E\lVert Y - Y'\rVert$ between the sample set and held-out data: zero exactly when the two distributions are equal, and computable in 2-D without a feature network (image papers use FID, which compares Inception-network features instead).

## The same model in PyTorch

The code below is a line-for-line PyTorch translation of the numpy toy. **It was not executed here** (PyTorch is not installed in the environment that wrote this site); the numpy version above is the one that produced the figures. Autograd replaces the hand-written backward pass, and [`torch.optim.Adam`](https://docs.pytorch.org/docs/stable/generated/torch.optim.Adam.html) replaces the manual Adam update.

```python
import math
import torch
import torch.nn as nn

T = 100
betas = torch.linspace(10 * 1e-4, 10 * 0.02, T)      # scaled linear
alphas = 1 - betas
alpha_bar = torch.cumprod(alphas, dim=0)

def time_embedding(t, dim=16):
    half = dim // 2
    freqs = torch.exp(-math.log(10000.0) * torch.arange(half) / half)
    ang = t.float()[:, None] * freqs[None, :]
    return torch.cat([torch.sin(ang), torch.cos(ang)], dim=1)

class EpsMLP(nn.Module):
    def __init__(self, emb=16, hidden=128):
        super().__init__()
        self.net = nn.Sequential(
            nn.Linear(2 + emb, hidden), nn.SiLU(),
            nn.Linear(hidden, hidden), nn.SiLU(),
            nn.Linear(hidden, hidden), nn.SiLU(),
            nn.Linear(hidden, 2))

    def forward(self, x, t):
        return self.net(torch.cat([x, time_embedding(t)], dim=1))

model = EpsMLP()
opt = torch.optim.Adam(model.parameters(), lr=1e-3)
loss_fn = nn.MSELoss(reduction='sum')                 # summed, then /batch
# data: float32 tensor of shape (8000, 2)

for step in range(20000):                             # Algorithm 1
    x0 = data[torch.randint(0, len(data), (256,))]
    t = torch.randint(1, T + 1, (256,))
    eps = torch.randn_like(x0)
    ab = alpha_bar[t - 1][:, None]
    xt = ab.sqrt() * x0 + (1 - ab).sqrt() * eps
    loss = loss_fn(model(xt, t), eps) / x0.shape[0]   # L_simple
    opt.zero_grad()
    loss.backward()
    opt.step()

@torch.no_grad()
def sample(n):                                        # Algorithm 2
    x = torch.randn(n, 2)
    for t in range(T, 0, -1):
        z = torch.randn_like(x) if t > 1 else torch.zeros_like(x)
        tt = torch.full((n,), t)
        a, ab, beta = alphas[t - 1], alpha_bar[t - 1], betas[t - 1]
        x = (x - beta / (1 - ab).sqrt() * model(x, tt)) / a.sqrt() + beta.sqrt() * z
    return x
```

Two details to match the numpy run exactly: the toy keeps the learning rate constant for the first 55% of training and then decays it linearly to $0.1\times$ at the last step (a [[4-pytorch-training/learning-rate-schedulers|`LambdaLR` scheduler]] does this), and it initializes every weight matrix from $\mathcal N(0, 2/\text{fan-in})$ with zero biases and scales the last layer's weights by 0.1, whereas [`nn.Linear`](https://docs.pytorch.org/docs/stable/generated/torch.nn.Linear.html) by default draws weights and biases uniformly from $\pm 1/\sqrt{\text{fan-in}}$. Neither matters for understanding the method.

> [!trap] Common mistakes
> - **Off-by-one in $t$.** The paper indexes steps $1..T$ and defines $\bar\alpha_t$ as the product up to $t$; arrays are 0-based, so $\bar\alpha_t$ is `alpha_bar[t - 1]`. Mixing the two shifts every coefficient by one step.
> - **Forgetting $z = 0$ at the last step.** Adding noise at $t = 1$ leaves visible grain in every sample.
> - **Changing $T$ without changing $\beta$.** With $T = 100$ and the paper's $\beta$ range, $\bar\alpha_T \approx 0.36$: $x_T$ is far from $\mathcal N(0, I)$ and sampling from pure noise fails. Keep $\bar\alpha_T \approx 0$ (scale the range, or use a cosine schedule).
> - **Reading the loss as a quality score.** $L_{\text{simple}}$ plateaus well above 0 (about 0.45 in the toy) because $\epsilon$ is unpredictable at large $t$; sample quality has to be measured on samples.
> - **Treating $\epsilon_\theta$ as a denoised image.** The network predicts the noise; the implied clean image is $\hat x_0 = (x_t - \sqrt{1-\bar\alpha_t}\,\epsilon_\theta)/\sqrt{\bar\alpha_t}$ (eq. 15), and it is blurry at large $t$.
> - **Notation clash with DDIM.** The DDIM paper writes $\alpha_t$ for what DDPM calls $\bar\alpha_t$; see [[8-diffusion-models/fast-sampling-ddim-and-ode-solvers|fast sampling]].

> [!question]- Check yourself
> 1. With the paper's schedule, what fraction of the original signal amplitude $\sqrt{\bar\alpha_t}$ remains at $t = 250$ and at $t = 1000$?
> 2. Show that $\tilde\mu_t$ becomes $\frac{1}{\sqrt{\alpha_t}}\bigl(x_t - \frac{\beta_t}{\sqrt{1-\bar\alpha_t}}\epsilon\bigr)$ when $x_0$ is written in terms of $x_t$ and $\epsilon$.
> 3. A DDPM with $T = 1000$ takes 40 ms per network evaluation on a batch. How long does one batch of samples take, and why can the loop not be parallelized?
> 4. Why does training not need to simulate the forward chain step by step?
>
> > [!solution]- Answers
> > 1. $\bar\alpha_{250} \approx 0.524$, so $\sqrt{\bar\alpha_{250}} \approx 0.72$; $\bar\alpha_{1000} \approx 4.0\times10^{-5}$, so $\sqrt{\bar\alpha_{1000}} \approx 0.0064$.
> > 2. Substitute $x_0 = (x_t - \sqrt{1-\bar\alpha_t}\epsilon)/\sqrt{\bar\alpha_t}$. The $x_t$ coefficient becomes $\frac{\beta_t}{\sqrt{\alpha_t}(1-\bar\alpha_t)} + \frac{\sqrt{\alpha_t}(1-\bar\alpha_{t-1})}{1-\bar\alpha_t} = \frac{\beta_t + \alpha_t - \bar\alpha_t}{\sqrt{\alpha_t}(1-\bar\alpha_t)} = \frac{1}{\sqrt{\alpha_t}}$, and the $\epsilon$ coefficient is $-\frac{\beta_t\sqrt{1-\bar\alpha_t}}{\sqrt{\alpha_t}(1-\bar\alpha_t)} = -\frac{\beta_t}{\sqrt{\alpha_t}\sqrt{1-\bar\alpha_t}}$ (using $\sqrt{\bar\alpha_{t-1}}/\sqrt{\bar\alpha_t} = 1/\sqrt{\alpha_t}$).
> > 3. $1000 \times 40$ ms $= 40$ s. Step $t-1$ needs $x_t$, which is the output of the previous evaluation, so the steps are sequential (the same bottleneck as decoding an [[7-transformers/sequence-to-sequence-rnns|RNN]] token by token).
> > 4. Because $q(x_t\mid x_0)$ is available in closed form: one Gaussian sample gives $x_t$ for any $t$ directly.

## PyTorch docs

- [`torch.nn.Module`](https://docs.pytorch.org/docs/stable/generated/torch.nn.Module.html), [`nn.Linear`](https://docs.pytorch.org/docs/stable/generated/torch.nn.Linear.html), [`nn.SiLU`](https://docs.pytorch.org/docs/stable/generated/torch.nn.SiLU.html), [`nn.Sequential`](https://docs.pytorch.org/docs/stable/generated/torch.nn.Sequential.html) — the noise predictor.
- [`torch.randn_like`](https://docs.pytorch.org/docs/stable/generated/torch.randn_like.html), [`torch.randint`](https://docs.pytorch.org/docs/stable/generated/torch.randint.html), [`torch.randn`](https://docs.pytorch.org/docs/stable/generated/torch.randn.html) — sampling $\epsilon$, $t$ and $x_T$.
- [`torch.linspace`](https://docs.pytorch.org/docs/stable/generated/torch.linspace.html), [`torch.cumprod`](https://docs.pytorch.org/docs/stable/generated/torch.cumprod.html) — the schedule $\beta_t$ and $\bar\alpha_t$.
- [`nn.MSELoss`](https://docs.pytorch.org/docs/stable/generated/torch.nn.MSELoss.html) — $L_{\text{simple}}$ (note its default `reduction='mean'` also divides by the dimension).
- [`torch.optim.Adam`](https://docs.pytorch.org/docs/stable/generated/torch.optim.Adam.html) and [`torch.no_grad`](https://docs.pytorch.org/docs/stable/generated/torch.no_grad.html) — training and sampling.

## References

- J. Ho, A. Jain, P. Abbeel. *Denoising Diffusion Probabilistic Models.* NeurIPS 2020. [arXiv:2006.11239](https://arxiv.org/abs/2006.11239) — eqs. 1–15, Algorithms 1–2, Tables 1–3 and Appendix B are the source of every DDPM number on this page.
- J. Sohl-Dickstein, E. Weiss, N. Maheswaranathan, S. Ganguli. *Deep Unsupervised Learning using Nonequilibrium Thermodynamics.* ICML 2015. [arXiv:1503.03585](https://arxiv.org/abs/1503.03585)
- A. Nichol, P. Dhariwal. *Improved Denoising Diffusion Probabilistic Models.* ICML 2021. [arXiv:2102.09672](https://arxiv.org/abs/2102.09672) — cosine schedule, learned variances.
- Y. Song, S. Ermon. *Generative Modeling by Estimating Gradients of the Data Distribution.* NeurIPS 2019. [arXiv:1907.05600](https://arxiv.org/abs/1907.05600)
- P. Vincent. *A Connection Between Score Matching and Denoising Autoencoders.* Neural Computation 23(7), 2011.
